A Sentence-by-Sentence Close Reading
Senior Literary Agent & Editor's Report

All 8,335 sentences examined and accounted for by number. 664 flagged with notes; the remainder
certified in per-chapter pass ranges. Sentence IDs (e.g. [20.211]) key every note to the numbered sentence
map of the manuscript.

Sygl — Close Reading · 1

SYGL — Senior Agent's Report: The Sentence-Level Read
Prepared after an exhaustive close reading of all 8,335 sentences of the manuscript — every sentence
examined, given a disposition, and accounted for by number. 664 sentences (8.0%) were flagged with a
note; 92 were singled out as standouts. The full sentence-by-sentence record follows this report, chapter
by chapter.

The bottom line
I would take this book on — after one targeted revision pass, and positioned as New Adult / crossover
romantic fantasy, not YA. The prose is the asset: this is a writer whose copy is mechanically clean
(grammar is the smallest flag category in the entire read — 20 flags out of 664), whose dialogue
differentiates, whose physical writing is precise, and whose restraint most debut novelists have to be
taught. The liability is also consistent, and it is not the writing: it is bookkeeping. In a novel whose plot
runs on stated rules — sygl ranks, war conditions, deadlines, ring terms — the rules drift at sentence level
in about a dozen places, and series readers are exactly the readers who catch drift. Nearly every fix on the
priority list below is one sentence long. That is the best possible shape for a revision letter: the problems
are small, specific, and cheap; the strengths are the expensive kind that can't be edited into existence.

What the sentence-level pass sees that a chapter read cannot
1. The verdict-then-proof habit (the book's signature flaw). TELLING accounts for 95 flags, and the
pattern is specific: the narration states a quality, then the scene demonstrates it anyway. The bare office is
certified three times in seven sentences (4.040, 4.042, 4.046); a feeling is graded at 5.104, 5.176 and 5.182
in the book's best early chapter; reactions are captioned after they land (6.179, 6.183). The draft does not
trust itself, and it should — the shown version is almost always stronger than the verdict. A revision pass
that simply deleted flagged verdict-sentences would lose almost no information and gain measurable pace
across all thirty chapters.
2. Systems nouns drift — and in this book, terminology is plot. The power system is denominated in
priests versus High Priests, and the words slip: Raizen's agent is a "follower" at 4.249 and a "high priest"
three sentences later (4.252); Bal expands the war rule to cover ordinary priests at 20.211, against its
precise statement at 18.182–18.183 and 19.227; the originator ranking reverses between 11 and 15.119;
Viridia's ring is restated at 14.129 with weaker terms than the jeweler's card gave it in Chapter Thirteen.
Individually these are slips. Collectively they are the one thing an acquiring editor will cite when they say
the worldbuilding "isn't quite controlled yet" — and for a rules-driven series, control of the rulebook is the
franchise.
3. The right palm. The sygl lives in the left palm; Bal's regrown hand is his left; and yet his blood exits
the right palm at 20.034, again at 21.122, again at 25.162 — while at 27.293–27.294 both hands finally
work in the same breath. Nobody on my read believes this is a typo anymore; it reads as an unstated
system. Either state the two-hand rule somewhere, or correct the three sentences. In a novel whose entire
premise is a hand, readers will audit the hands. (They will also find 17.157 — Bal juggling three stones on
muscle memory "learned as a boy," with the one hand he was born without a second of. One line fixes it. It
is the sharpest single-sentence catch in the book.)
4. Anachronism leaks at peak moments. Twice in 8,335 sentences the prose reaches outside its world:
the temple floor becomes "solid concrete" at 1.164, and Bal experiences Amelia's memories "like

Sygl — Close Reading · 2

somebody watching a movie" at 28.195. Two instances is not carelessness — it's a 99.98% clean record —
but both occur at high-attention moments (the temple's first description; the climax power-up's key
perception), where a leak costs the most. A find-and-fix pass on modern-world vocabulary is an
afternoon's work.
5. Rules get asserted hardest exactly where they're newest. When a mechanism first appears at a
moment of maximum convenience, the prose argues: the purification rune's rule is stated absolutely, three
times in three sentences (25.168–25.170), at the instant it saves Viridia; Amelia's engineered proximity is
overstated as constant at 10.110; the conversion mechanism is vaguest (8.088) where it matters most.
Readers feel this as convenience even when the mechanism is legitimate. The fix is uniform and cheap:
bound the rule once, earlier, and let the scene spend it without commentary.
6. The agency pattern — the book's real editorial question, visible only at sentence level. Across thirty
chapters, a cluster recurs: Bal arranges outcomes for people he loves, and the narration absolves him in the
same breath. He presumes Viridia won't mind being maneuvered (10.115); his confession breakthrough
rests on a misstated account of what Amelia told him (12.113); he silences Amelia and narrates her trauma
for her in the scene that is about her (14.066–14.091); he improvises a simultaneous double conversion
against instructions with no stated motive (19.162); and at 30.157 he stands in the doorframe gazing at
"those three beautiful women" on the threshold of a final line about belonging. Taken one at a time, each is
a flag. Taken together, they are either the book's most interesting flaw — a kind boy becoming a wartime
arranger, Amelia "rubbing off on him," as he himself notices at 20.154 — or an unintentional savior
pattern that reviewers will name if the book doesn't. A senior editor's one structural demand: decide, and
put the decision on the page. One crack of self-awareness, one cost, converts this from liability to theme.

The prose, measured
The manuscript's statistical profile confirms what the read felt like. Average sentence: 10.9 words. Just
under a third of all sentences (29%) run five words or fewer — this is a dialogue-and-beat texture, not a
descriptive one, and it is why the book reads fast despite its length. Chapter One is the outlier (16.0 words
average, the book's most "written" chapter) and the school and war chapters settle to 9.5–11. The flag rate
by category is the healthiest distribution I could ask for in a debut: CLARITY 179, LOGIC 96, TELLING
95, REPETITION 73, IMAGE 59, RHYTHM 47, DIALOGUE 35, CONTINUITY 33, POV 27,
GRAMMAR 20. Read that list bottom-up: the mechanics are nearly clean; the flags concentrate in
judgment calls — clarity of reference, logic inside the sentence, trust in the scene. Those are revision-pass
problems, not rewrite problems.
The standouts matter too: 92 sentences were pulled as genuinely excellent — the pigeon's footsteps in the
courtyard silence (Ch6), Viridia's hair as an instrument panel carrying an entire breakdown without naming
an emotion (Ch26), the casualty arithmetic that argues the Londra mission better than any speech (Ch22).
This writer's best sentences are competitive with published work in the category. They are not rare
accidents; they recur in every third chapter.

Category and market
Category: New Adult / crossover romantic fantasy. Bal is eighteen and the book must say so on its
opening pages — currently the fact is buried in a subordinate clause at 3.013, three chapters in, while the
orphanage, softball, and school textures pull a sample reader toward YA and the erotic charge around
Amelia, the conversion consent architecture (Ch19), and the closing household framing (30.151–30.157)

Sygl — Close Reading · 3

pull the other way. Neither pull is a problem. The ambiguity is. Pick the shelf, put the age in Chapter One,
and let the query letter say plainly what the book is.
Positioning: academy fantasy with a distinctive caste mechanic (status inscribed in the left palm — lead
with this, not with "magic school"), a goddess-familiar who is the book's most castable character, and a
found-family engine that the ending genuinely earns. Series architecture is real: the Dawn guild supplies an
episodic job engine school scenes alone couldn't, "Sygls Best" is a brandable team name, and the
unresolved threads (Toma converted, the blood elemental, the traceless abduction — which the prose at
27.149–27.187 deliberately stages as mystery rather than oversight) are legitimate book-two doors,
provided book two pays the abduction. Sample strength: Chapter Five converts a partial request into a
full read; Chapter Six (the Claiming) is the set piece to excerpt; Chapter Twenty-Six is the chapter to quote
in a pitch letter.
What an acquiring editor's letter will contain (so none of it surprises you): dramatize one of Amelia's
memories and one pre-duel technique (Ch28's withholding is the ending's only structural debt); plant the
blood-touches-bond beat before the climax so Toma's conversion reads as fate rather than rescue (29.239,
29.327); compress Chapter Thirty's recap by a third; and fix the rulebook items below. Everything else in
their letter will be praise for the voice.

The priority fix list (sentence-level, in order)
1. Bal's age into Chapter One — currently first disclosed, buried, at 3.013.
2. [20.211] — restore the war rule to its 18.182–18.183 wording ("High Priest" only), or re-state the rule
everywhere.
3. [18.095] — the dropped negative that reverses the pogrom passage's meaning. The most urgent single
word in the book.
4. [17.157] — the two-handed juggling learned by a one-handed boy.
5. [22.047] — "Converted. Last night." The conversion was weeks earlier (Ch19), said to priests
positioned to know.
6. [24.410] — a full cauldron of poison cannot be spooned into a full cask of brandy; draw brandy off first.
7. [6.195] / [13.470] — the surname Norheart: Amelia speaks it publicly before Bal has it, and Soren
knows it unintroduced. Own it as a plant or fix both.
8. [5.157]–[5.158] — the broken pronoun in the feeding request, the book's most delicate early scene.
9. [15.119] — reconcile the originator ranking with Chapter Eleven.
10. [14.129] — restate Viridia's ring terms exactly as the jeweler's card gave them.
11. The 72-hour flowers ([17.037]–[17.038]) — honor the deadline in Chapter Eighteen's montage with
one line, or cut the clock in Seventeen. The book's other clocks (the poison timing across Ch25–27 locks
correctly) prove the standard is achievable.
12. [28.195] — replace the "movie" simile with an in-world frame; [1.164] — "concrete" to cut stone.
13. [12.113] — decide whether Bal's diary lie is characterization; if yes, let it cost something later.

Sygl — Close Reading · 4

14. [30.157] — the doorframe gaze: intend the household framing or cut the sentence. Beside it, the final
line itself needs no help at all — it is earned, and it is the sentence this book will be remembered by.

Verdict on readiness
Not query-ready today; query-ready after one disciplined pass at the fourteen items above plus a
verdict-sentence trim — realistically days of work, not months, because the fixes are surgical and the base
prose is sound. My honest assessment of the manuscript's standing: premise, voice, and emotional engine
are above the debut median for this category; systems bookkeeping is below it; and the gap between those
two is exactly the gap a good revision closes. The chapter-by-chapter record that follows is the working
document for that pass — every flagged sentence carries its location, its category, and the direction of its
fix.
— Acheron, acting as senior literary agent & editor

Sygl — Close Reading · 5

The Close Reading, Chapter by Chapter
Each chapter file records: its coverage accounting (flagged sentences + pass ranges tiling every sentence
ID exactly once), the flagged sentences with category and fix direction, standout sentences, the editor's
chapter notes, and the agent's notes.

Sygl — Close Reading · 6

Chapter One: The Roof and the Flicker — Sentence-by-Sentence
Close Reading
Coverage
199 sentences total; 28 flagged; 171 passed. Every ID from 1.001 through 1.199 is accounted for exactly
once, either below or in the pass ranges.
Pass: 1.001–1.012, 1.014–1.023, 1.025–1.029, 1.031–1.038, 1.040–1.047, 1.049–1.060, 1.062, 1.064–1.071, 1.073–1.075, 1.077–1.085,
1.087–1.098, 1.100–1.102, 1.104–1.110, 1.112–1.118, 1.120–1.128, 1.130–1.138, 1.140–1.147, 1.149–1.150, 1.152–1.153, 1.156,
1.158–1.163, 1.165–1.166, 1.168–1.172, 1.174–1.179, 1.181–1.183, 1.185–1.189, 1.191–1.196, 1.198–1.199

Flagged sentences
[1.013] “It had been built sometime in the 1700s, but…” — RHYTHM — The sentence carries age,
condition, height, materials, and the roof leak in one long coordinate chain, so the orphanage arrives as an
inventory rather than a shaped first impression. Fix: Break after “building that age” and let the
brick-and-leak description stand as its own sentence.
[1.024] “For some reason the stump always itched when he…” — CLARITY — The relative clause makes
“odd enough” explain both the itch and Bal’s silence, but the construction is slack and “for some reason” is
a placeholder rather than a perception. Fix: Tighten to the observed pattern—stump, carvings, itch—and
drop the explanatory filler.
[1.030] “A sygl appeared in the palm sometime in childhood,…” — CLARITY — The foundational
exposition says only “in the palm,” omitting the left/right fact on which Bal’s exclusion depends until
1.034. A first-time reader has to revise the rule immediately after learning it. Fix: Put “left palm” here and
let 1.034 land as Bal’s personal consequence rather than a correction.
[1.039] “So at least he had that.…” — CLARITY — “That” has no clean antecedent: it could mean having
any magic, working harder, or being unlike the wholly unmagical children. The consolation is emotionally
clear but grammatically blurred. Fix: Name the consolation—his own usable magic—rather than pointing
back to it.
[1.048] “Which was why, on this particular afternoon, he was…” — TELLING — The sentence is a
dependent fragment and announces that the attempt “could kill him” before Bal’s plan has earned that level
of stated danger. It editorializes risk the subsequent methodical preparation could imply. Fix: End the
section on the fact of the attempt; reserve mortal danger for the measured gap and failed alternatives.
[1.061] “Bal had sat there with the apple halfway to…” — LOGIC — An apple does not visibly brown in
the few suspended seconds the syntax implies; the detail unintentionally stretches the flicker into a long
trance. Fix: Use the arrested gesture without the browning, or establish that he remained staring for
minutes.
[1.063] “The roof hadn't flickered again, though he'd watched it…” — RHYTHM — “Watched,” “eyes
watered,” “but,” and “knew what he’d seen” crowd one long sentence, flattening the distinction between
repeated vigils and present certainty. Fix: Split the daily returns from the conviction; the short assertion
will hit harder.
[1.072] “He had measured the gap with his eyes about…” — RHYTHM — At roughly fifty words, the
measurement, imagined dismemberment, and “second story” joke compete in one breath. The wit is good,

Sygl — Close Reading · 7

but the sentence makes the reader unpack it instead of falling with Bal. Fix: Give the fatal landing its own
sentence and keep the staff-story joke as the closer.
[1.076] “Making himself lighter had sounded clever until he read…” — LOGIC — Making himself lighter
would not by itself explain why stopping is especially hard; the objection confuses weight, momentum,
and control. As written, the rejection feels invented to eliminate an option. Fix: Specify the spell’s actual
drawback—drift, poor braking, or inability to steer in wind.
[1.086] “Earlier in the year, the letter had come from…” — RHYTHM — The letter, Fredrick’s
distinction, and the whole orphanage’s send-off are compressed into one overloaded sentence. It is
intelligible, but the institutional importance of a school letter gets no room to register. Fix: Separate the
letter’s arrival from the public farewell tableau.
[1.099] “No school would take you without one, no matter…” — POV — The narration briefly shifts from
close third person into generic second person—“No school would take you”—which loosens Bal’s point of
view at a moment of personal exclusion. Fix: Keep the rule in Bal’s frame: no school would take him.
[1.103] “It was the kindest thing anyone had done for…” — TELLING — “The kindest thing anyone had
done for Bal in years” appraises a scene that has already proved its kindness through the books, the string,
and Fredrick’s refusal to sell them. The summary dilutes the evidence. Fix: Cut the appraisal or replace it
with one concrete thing Bal does with the gift afterward.
[1.111] “Lifting and carrying, the book said, for objects of…” — LOGIC — “Objects of moderate size” is
too vague for the spell’s first hard limit, especially when Bal immediately uses it to move a rug bearing his
whole body. The sentence leaves unclear whether the load, the rug, or both fall within the rule. Fix: Give
the textbook a usable capacity or warn explicitly that a passenger exceeds the intended load.
[1.119] “He'd lain awake afterward staring at the ceiling, his…” — IMAGE — “His heart thudding in his
ears” is stock physiological shorthand in an otherwise freshly observed practice sequence. It tells
excitement generically rather than extending the tactile attention used for the rug and floorboards. Fix:
Replace the cliché with a sensation specific to sustained levitation or to Bal’s stump.
[1.129] “When he tried to push it down that arm,…” — RHYTHM — The blockage mechanics are
important, but the sentence layers “when,” “then,” “until,” and “somewhere around” until the physical
route of the magic becomes harder to feel. The exposition arrives just as the action should tighten. Fix:
Divide the journey at the elbow; let the blockage stop a shorter second sentence.
[1.139] “Power flowed out of the end of his arm…” — LOGIC — Bal has already said the incantation in
1.132, so “as he finished saying the words” repeats a completed beat. The very long sentence also tries to
cover speech, flow, ascent, stopping, and stability at once. Fix: Begin this sentence at the power flow and
remove the duplicated speech timing.
[1.148] “One second he was hovering over the orphanage roof,…” — RHYTHM — The sentence stacks
hovering, slate movement, empty air, and the drop to the vegetable beds in one accelerating chain. The
speed is intentional, but the syntax blurs Bal’s exact position at the instant of danger. Fix: Break when the
slates slide away; the unsupported drop deserves isolated emphasis.
[1.151] “He clamped his hand on the carpet's edge so…” — RHYTHM — Five coordinated
actions—clamping, not looking, looking, seeing, deciding—are chained with commas and “and” until the
joke depends on parsing a long inventory. The breathlessness is apt, but the sequence loses spatial clarity.
Fix: Split before the look down and let the map-like garden view open a new sentence.
Sygl — Close Reading · 8

[1.154] “He leaned back without thinking and the carpet slowed…” — LOGIC — The carpet slows
because Bal leans back, but no control mechanism has been established for posture or intention beyond
willing it to move in 1.145. The intuitive piloting needs one earlier cue. Fix: Plant during practice that the
rug answers shifts of weight as well as thought.
[1.155] “What Bal didn't expect was that when he tried…” — CLARITY — The nested
construction—“didn’t expect was that when he tried… he would find”—delays the discovery and makes
the tense feel hypothetical rather than immediate. Fix: Recast directly: “Then he tried to land, and there
was no roof.”
[1.157] “There was a cool tingle over his skin, gone…” — IMAGE — The daylight “changed color” is a
potentially important threshold effect, but the sentence never says what color it becomes. The omission
makes the transition feel summarized at exactly the moment sensory precision matters. Fix: Specify the
shift—cooler, gold, shadowless—or replace color with the changed quality Bal actually registers.
[1.164] “He kept giving the same command, lower and lower,…” — CONTINUITY — The temple floor
becomes “solid concrete,” although 1.056 calls it pale stone and the surrounding description consistently
presents cut stone. “Concrete” also imports a modern material note the ancient temple has not earned. Fix:
Use “stone floor” unless the concrete is a deliberate clue about the temple’s construction.
[1.167] “There was no smoke anywhere, and no smell of…” — IMAGE — “Something spiced” withholds
the one sensory particular that would make the smokeless braziers strange and memorable. It gestures at a
smell rather than rendering it. Fix: Name the spice-like note—clove, resin, cinnamon, or another
world-appropriate scent.
[1.173] “"Locos cantis," he murmured as he stepped off the…” — CLARITY — “Locos cantis” appears
without preparation and its jokey pseudo-Latin sits uneasily beside the formal “Levimotorcorpus.” At
sentence level, the reader cannot tell whether this is a learned counterspell, Bal’s invention, or a joke
breaking the magic system’s register. Fix: Establish the release phrase in the spell-book passage or give it
the same linguistic treatment as the main incantation.
[1.180] “High stone pillars marched down both sides of the…” — IMAGE — Pillars “marched” is a
familiar architectural metaphor, and “plain and grand” names the effect instead of sharpening the visual
evidence around it. The sentence is serviceable but generic in a set-piece entrance. Fix: Keep one metaphor
and add a scale or surface detail unique to this temple.
[1.184] “A broad shaft of afternoon light came slanting down…” — IMAGE — Dust motes “turned” in
the light is slightly off as physical observation; they seem to rotate only because the verb lacks the drift or
suspension the image needs. Fix: Use “hung,” “drifted,” or “turned slowly” with a directional cue.
[1.190] “He was still wondering how he could possibly be…” — RHYTHM — The long subordinate
tail—“when someone answered the question for him”—withholds the voice through several layers of
wondering. Suspense is the aim, but the syntax announces the interruption before it lands. Fix: End on
Bal’s unanswered question and let 1.191 arrive unannounced.
[1.197] “But the words had been close enough that he'd…” — POV — “Plainly enjoying themselves”
confidently assigns amusement to an unseen speaker and uses a plural reflexive for a singular “whoever.”
The interpretation may be right, but the narration reaches beyond Bal’s evidence in the instant. Fix:
Ground the amusement in the whisper’s tone or Bal’s felt response rather than certifying it.

Sygl — Close Reading · 9

Standout sentences
• [1.034] “A sygl came up in the left palm…” — The premise is reduced to anatomy and exclusion in one

clean blow; everything Bal lacks is in the sentence.
• [1.123] “That also meant there was nobody to see him fall…” — The apparent reassurance reverses

perfectly into danger, and the dry logic is pure Bal.
• [1.198] “Bal swallowed, and wished he’d brought a better plan…” — Bathos used exactly right: two

cheese sandwiches measure the distance between a schoolboy expedition and an ancient temple.

Editor's chapter notes
The prose is strongest in procedural sequences. Bal weighs bad options, practices on a sock, a pillow, a
book, a chair, and only then risks the rug; the sentences lengthen during preparation and contract at
moments of decision. That rhythm makes the flight legible. The chapter’s characteristic weakness is not
incompetence but over-compression at peaks: 1.139, 1.148, and 1.151 all let several consequential actions
share one breath, just when a break would increase force.
Short declaratives are used well for exclusion and resolve—1.034 through 1.039, and 1.160 through
1.163—but the narrator sometimes explains an effect after the scene has already produced it (1.103). Bal’s
voice is dry and practical, especially in the gap measurement at 1.072 and the sandwich joke at 1.198. The
dialogue with Fredrick is natural because neither boy names the injustice directly; by contrast, the
exposition around sygls occasionally turns encyclopedic, particularly when the rule’s crucial left-palm
placement is initially omitted at 1.030.
Repeated constructions are controlled rather than compulsive: inventories, options tested and rejected, and
physical checks recur because Bal thinks procedurally. The recurring abstractions to watch are
“something” formulations and stock body language—“something spiced” at 1.167 and the heart in the ears
at 1.119. Showing generally beats telling; the exceptions matter because the surrounding standard is
concrete.

Agent's notes
This is a commercially legible opening: a disabled orphan, a forbidden sealed temple, a visible magical
caste system, and a first chapter that ends with a voice answering him. The hook is not merely the premise
but the causal chain—Bal is excluded because he lacks the organ on which status literally appears, and
curiosity gives him a way to act anyway. An acquiring editor would call the voice accessible, the central
lack easy to pitch, and the chapter-one inciting incident properly early.
The submission risk is positioning. Bal reads young for much of the chapter because his age is never stated
here; the school, dormitory, sandwiches, and orphanage texture can pull the manuscript toward middle
grade even though later material requires an older YA/crossover read. Put the age on the page. A second
concern is premise protection: flying-carpet and magic-school furniture invites easy comparisons, but the
left-palm caste rule and the sealed “mausoleum” are distinctive enough if marketing copy leads with them
rather than with the school.

Chapter Two: The Queen in the Temple — Sentence-by-Sentence
Close Reading
Coverage
304 sentences total; 31 flagged; 273 passed. Every ID from 2.001 through 2.304 is accounted for exactly
once, either below or in the pass ranges.
Pass: 2.001–2.006, 2.009–2.010, 2.012–2.015, 2.018–2.021, 2.023–2.036, 2.038–2.063, 2.065–2.082, 2.084, 2.087–2.091, 2.093–2.098,
2.100–2.101, 2.103–2.110, 2.112–2.124, 2.126–2.150, 2.152–2.168, 2.170–2.214, 2.216–2.223, 2.225–2.226, 2.228–2.231, 2.233–2.239,
2.241–2.243, 2.245–2.246, 2.248–2.259, 2.261–2.263, 2.265–2.267, 2.269–2.270, 2.272–2.295, 2.297–2.300, 2.302–2.304

Flagged sentences
[2.007] “Bal guessed she was about his own age, though…” — CLARITY — The comparison to Bal’s
“own age” does not actually establish either age, and Bal’s age has still not been stated in the manuscript.
It also fixes Amelia visually as a peer while the scene insists she is ancient. Fix: State Bal’s age outright in
Chapter One; here, separate apparent age from carried age with a concrete incongruity.
[2.008] “She cocked her head and studied him with frank,…” — IMAGE — The cat-on-the-doorstep
simile imports something wet, bedraggled, and vulnerable into Amelia’s controlled entrance. Her frank
interest is clear, but the comparison works against the authority she is establishing. Fix: Choose an
observer image that preserves curiosity without diminishing her command.
[2.011] “It was a low voice with a laugh tucked…” — IMAGE — A laugh “tucked away somewhere
inside” a voice is familiar figurative shorthand. It gives Amelia charm, but by a route the chapter uses
more than once for vocal texture. Fix: Let pitch, timing, or Bal’s physical response carry the amusement in
this first exchange.
[2.016] “Her skin was pale as candle wax against all…” — IMAGE — “Pale as candle wax” is a stock
Gothic comparison, and it arrives in a beauty catalogue of rubies, black hair, black dress, and skin. The
sentence contributes color contrast but little that is specific to Amelia. Fix: Replace the conventional simile
with the way firelight behaves on her skin or another observed particular.
[2.017] “She wasn't skinny, but she wasn't fat either; she…” — POV — Bal’s assessment—“exactly the
right amount of curves,” with the dress making “no secret of them”—turns the first full look at Amelia into
a sexual appraisal. Combined with the unresolved ages in 2.007, it creates an avoidable category and
consent problem at their introduction. Fix: Signal attraction through Bal losing his prepared speech or
noticing one detail, rather than grading her body.
[2.022] “At a loss for words, which was a first…” — TELLING — “Which was a first for Bal” certifies a
lifelong superlative the novel has not established. It also restates the wordlessness the surrounding short
sentences have just shown. Fix: Cut the claim, or ground it in a specific contrast with how Bal normally
talks his way out of trouble.
[2.037] “That, at least, he could answer, once his tongue…” — IMAGE — A tongue coming “unstuck” is
serviceable but canned, particularly after the more specific image of questions gathering behind teeth in the
same conversational beat. Fix: Use the physical action already present—breath, swallow, first
word—without the stock release metaphor.

Sygl — Close Reading · 11

[2.064] “A thin film of dust lay on them.…” — LOGIC — The benches seem to be created from the air,
yet arrive with a film of dust on them. That detail quietly changes the feat from creation to summoning or
relocation, and the text does not acknowledge the difference. Fix: Remove the dust if she creates them, or
make the dust evidence that she pulled existing temple furniture from elsewhere.
[2.083] “Bal took in the two rows of pillars, the…” — REPETITION — Bal’s seated survey repeats the
pillars, braziers, sunlight, and statue already established in Chapter One and again at his landing. The
sentence delays the genuinely new observations collected in 2.085–2.087. Fix: Begin with what sitting still
changes—smoke, fuel, cleanliness—rather than re-inventorying the whole hall.
[2.085] “The braziers burned low and steady and gave off…” — REPETITION — The smokeless braziers
were already described in Chapter One, including the absence of burning wood as a smell. Re-presenting
the point as a fresh discovery makes Bal’s attention seem to reset between chapters. Fix: Keep only the
newly visible fact that there is no wood in the braziers.
[2.086] “The floor didn't have a speck of dirt on…” — REPETITION — The spotless floor repeats 1.181
almost directly. Bal’s next question—who does the sweeping—is the new beat; this sentence spends a full
stop arriving back at known information. Fix: Compress the repetition into the sweeping inference.
[2.092] “This one put a little crease between those perfect…” — IMAGE — “Perfect eyebrows” is generic
idealization rather than observation. Coming after the rubies, hair, skin, and curves, it makes Amelia sound
assembled from romance-copy attributes. Fix: Give the brow a movement or asymmetry Bal can actually
notice when her expression changes.
[2.099] “The Queen of the vampires was the one you…” — TELLING — The sentence explains that the
Queen was worst and supposedly real, but its paired “because” clauses lecture the reader on
dormitory-story hierarchy instead of dramatizing Bal’s recognition. Fix: Trust “Queen of the vampires”
and Bal’s reaction; add only the one fact needed about her supposed reality.
[2.102] “His voice came out embarrassingly high.…” — TELLING — “Embarrassingly” labels Bal’s
response after “His voice came out” has already made the pitch shift available to the reader. The adverb
performs the embarrassment for him. Fix: Cut the adverb or add a failed attempt to recover his normal
register.
[2.111] “Bal scrambled to his feet and began backing away…” — RHYTHM — Bal’s retreat is crowded
with simultaneous safeguards—backing, stepping, watching, hand raised, and an aside on the hand’s
uselessness. The sentence is clear but cumbersome for a moment that should feel reactive. Fix: Break after
the first careful step; let the raised hand and its futility follow.
[2.125] “Bal wasn't sure that was the comfort she seemed…” — TELLING — The narration explains that
Amelia’s “I won’t bite—not without asking” is not as comforting as she thinks. The joke and Bal’s
hesitation already carry that judgment. Fix: Delete the gloss and let his failure to sit do the work.
[2.151] “At her words, Balthazaar heard the truth.…” — TELLING — “At her words, Balthazaar heard
the truth” makes the narrator guarantee Amelia’s claim rather than dramatizing Bal’s recognition. The
novel needs his conviction, not authorial certification, because Amelia’s reliability remains a live question.
Fix: Recast as Bal’s bodily certainty or recognition, preserving the possibility that he is persuaded rather
than objectively informed.
[2.169] “If a baby's born showing a blood sygl, the…” — CONTINUITY — Chapter One establishes that
a sygl appears sometime in childhood, often overnight; this sentence introduces babies “born showing” a
Sygl — Close Reading · 12

blood sygl without marking blood as an exception. The policy horror lands, but the developmental rule
wobbles. Fix: Specify that blood sygls can declare at birth, unlike the usual childhood emergence.
[2.215] “He meant it literally.…” — TELLING — “He meant it literally” explains the idiom just before
the bat transformation demonstrates it. The aside briefly steps outside the scene’s momentum to make sure
the reader gets the joke. Fix: Cut the sentence; the swarm is the punchline.
[2.224] “Then the bats poured back together over the bench,…” — RHYTHM — Three coordinated
verbs—poured, folded, and the delayed appearance of Amelia—make the reassembly less crisp than the
explosive dispersal. The transformation’s final snap is softened by syntax. Fix: End the movement on the
bats folding in; give Amelia’s restored stillness a separate sentence.
[2.227] “A single tiny black wisp drifted down past his…” — CLARITY — The “single tiny black wisp”
is visually suggestive but materially unclear: smoke, hair, membrane, and magical residue all read
differently. Its vanishing closes the image before Bal or the reader knows what was seen. Fix: Anchor the
wisp to the swarm—feather, ash, or shadow—if the ambiguity is not an intended clue.
[2.232] “Her jaw was still set hard, and her fingers…” — CLARITY — Her knuckles “showed” is
incomplete as an image: showed white, showed through the fabric, or simply became visible are different
effects. The sentence reaches for intensity but omits the visible fact. Fix: Name what the grip does to skin
or cloth.
[2.240] “Even without my magic, you're still powerful in your…” — CLARITY — “Even without my
magic” is ambiguous in Amelia’s mouth: she could mean without her sygl, without her assistance, or
without the blood connection she has just identified. The distinction matters because she is assessing Bal’s
independent power. Fix: Name the excluded thing—her mark, her help, or her blood.
[2.244] “He'd said it to himself so many times that…” — IMAGE — Words worn flat “like directions to
the bathroom” is tonally and spatially jarring: Bal’s formulation is about reciting an exclusion until it loses
feeling, not about a memorized route. The simile adds a mundane joke where the beat needs worn-down
resignation. Fix: Compare the sentence to a prayer, warning, or dormitory rule he has repeated until it
stopped sounding like language.
[2.247] “The firelight caught them as she did, and now…” — POV — Bal has been studying Amelia’s
face at close range for the entire interview, so only “now” seeing that her eyes are red reads as authorial
withholding rather than discovery. Firelight has caught her rubies repeatedly; why it reveals her irises only
here needs a mechanism. Fix: Reveal the color at her first direct look, or make the narrowing itself newly
expose what was shadowed.
[2.260] “Bal found that he couldn't look away either.…” — IMAGE — “Found that he couldn’t look
away” is a familiar compulsion formula and a filter construction. It states the spellbound effect instead of
showing Bal fail to break eye contact. Fix: Give him one attempted glance elsewhere that does not happen.
[2.264] “Nobody in those stories ever asked what anything meant…” — CLARITY — “What anything
meant” is too vague for an otherwise acute observation about story victims failing to ask questions.
“Anything” blurs whether he means the bargain, the price, the bond, or the terms. Fix: Name the unasked
thing: what she wanted, or what the promise would cost.
[2.268] “Amelia laughed and sat down beside him, and the…” — IMAGE — “The spell of the moment
broke” invokes an unspecified figurative spell immediately after literal magic has dominated the scene.
The cliché also risks confusing Amelia’s influence with enchantment. Fix: Use her laugh, movement, or
Sygl — Close Reading · 13

shoulder contact—the actual agents already in the next sentences.
[2.271] “She swung her feet beneath the hem of her…” — POV — Comparing Amelia’s swinging feet to
“a girl let out of lessons early” infantilizes an ancient originator at the moment she is negotiating a bond.
With Bal’s age unstated and her body already sexualized, the simile intensifies the manuscript’s
age-register confusion. Fix: Keep the restless feet but compare them to impatience or confinement, not
schoolgirl release.
[2.296] “Going to a Sygldry school, as Fredrick had, climbing…” — RHYTHM — Bal’s school fantasy
runs as a verbless catalogue—going, climbing, waving—long enough that its grammar starts to feel
accidental rather than dreamlike. The emotional list is strong, but the sentence loses forward pressure
before the practical objection arrives. Fix: Give the central fantasy a finite verb and reserve fragments for
its tail.
[2.301] “"I'd need a hand to put it on.…” — CONTINUITY — Bal asks how he can “get your sygl back”
and says he needs a hand “to put it on,” but Amelia has just told him the connection remains in his blood
despite amputation. The sentence confuses restoring a visible mark, regrowing a hand, and recovering a
lost bond. Fix: Have Bal ask how the mark can show or settle without a palm, preserving Amelia’s
distinction between connection and hand.

Standout sentences
• [2.087] “Bal wondered, briefly, who did the sweeping.” — A perfect character beat: confronted by an

ancient temple, Bal’s practical mind finds the domestic impossibility.
• [2.156] “Someone had bricked up a door in him…” — The architectural metaphor converts the wrist

blockage from exposition into injury and theft; it is the chapter’s emotional thesis in one image.
• [2.304] “Are you thirsty?” — Three ordinary words become a cliffhanger because the reader knows

whose wrist, what liquid, and what bargain they imply.

Editor's chapter notes
This chapter is a two-person interview carried almost entirely by dialogue, and its rhythm comes from
asymmetry: Bal explains in breathless runs, Amelia answers in short, polished turns that reset the power
balance. When the prose follows that contrast, it is lively. When narration intervenes to certify the
effect—Bal’s embarrassment at 2.102, the comfort gloss at 2.125, the “truth” at 2.151—it weakens lines
that were already doing the work.
Amelia’s dialogue is theatrical by design. Some formulations sound written rather than spoken (“Let them
call me a myth with me standing in the middle of their courtyard”), but that is plausibly the voice of a
temple goddess performing herself after centuries alone. Bal’s speech is less aphoristic and therefore
grounds her. The risk is sameness in the descriptive prose around them: rubies catch firelight, skirts are
handled, mouths drop open, braziers flare with emotion, and Amelia is repeatedly assembled from
superlative physical details. The repetitions are individually small; cumulatively they make the narration
more conventional than the characters.
Filter language is frequent—Bal guesses, notices, sees, watches, and looks—but close third person remains
coherent because Amelia is usually the observed object and Bal’s inferences are characterful. The
chapter’s more serious prose habit is delayed or withheld basic information for effect: his age, her eye
color at 2.247, and the exact status of his surviving blood connection at 2.301. Those are not mysteries the
Sygl — Close Reading · 14

scene is investigating; they are facts the point-of-view character already has access to, so withholding them
feels editorial rather than organic.

Agent's notes
Chapter Two supplies the marketable scene: the sealed temple contains not a monster but a displaced
goddess with a grievance, a sense of humor, and an offer that solves the hero’s exclusion while implicating
him in her revenge. Amelia immediately becomes the book’s most castable and quotable figure. An
acquiring editor would see a strong second-chapter hook and a relationship engine capable of carrying a
series.
The red flags are equally clear. Bal’s age remains unstated while Amelia is introduced as visually his peer,
physically appraised, tactile, and called into a quasi-romantic bond dynamic; she also calls him “a boy.” If
this is YA, the age and power asymmetry must be made unmistakable and handled deliberately. If it is
adult fantasy or romantasy, Bal’s orphanage-and-schoolboy presentation risks promising the wrong
category. The chapter also spends its whole length in one interview: the revelations justify it, but
commercial pacing depends on Amelia’s charisma because there is no external interruption or competing
objective.

[3.001] “Chapter Three A New Hand As it turned out,…” — TELLING — The chapter opens in

that removes both actors from the decisive act. Fix: Start at the wrist or Bal’s hesitation and let the method
become clear as he drinks.
[3.004] “He'd read every book Fredrick had left him, some…” — REPETITION — The absent-book joke
has already been made by 3.002 and 3.003; this sentence proves it a third time with the full reading history.
The extra evidence slows a sharp opening. Fix: Keep one version of the joke and move directly to
Amelia’s lack of ceremony.
[3.013] “He thought about the school too, and Fredrick waving…” — CLARITY — Bal’s
age—eighteen—is finally disclosed here, buried inside a list of thoughts and phrased as the duration of the
itch. A fact that determines how the reader should register the orphanage, Amelia’s physical attention, and
Bal’s legal and emotional agency should not arrive as an incidental subordinate detail in Chapter Three.
Fix: State his age plainly in Chapter One; this sentence can then focus on school, Fredrick, and the itch.
[3.017] “It went down his throat hot and fierce, far…” — IMAGE — Heat is successively a taste, “hot and
fierce,” “far too strong,” and then a “slow warm wave.” The synesthesia is promising, but the intensity
drops from overpowering to soothing within the same route through his body, muddying the drink’s
danger. Fix: Keep one temperature trajectory—either escalating heat or a fierce entry that settles into
warmth—and mark the transition.
[3.021] “When Bal next checked, the cut had closed over,…” — CLARITY — “When Bal next checked”
skips an unspecified interval between Amelia withdrawing her arm and the cut being perfectly closed. In a
scene otherwise attentive to seconds and sensations, the temporal compression makes her healing feel
elided rather than instantaneous. Fix: Anchor the check to the first burn or his next breath if the closure is
meant to be immediate.
[3.025] “There was no point pretending otherwise; it hurt badly,…” — REPETITION — This sentence
restates 3.024’s “It hurt” with the stock intensifier “hurt badly” and a conversational throat-clearing clause.
The repetition does not increase the pain; it explains that the prior sentence was sincere. Fix: Cut this
sentence or replace it with the pain’s specific quality, which 3.025 currently postpones.
[3.026] “But Bal had always had a high tolerance for…” — TELLING — Bal’s high pain tolerance is

preceding chapters. It functions as a convenient authorial permit to keep the scene controlled. Fix: Plant
the trait earlier through the stump, roof work, or an injury he refuses to make a fuss about.
[3.030] “The ache peaked, held for a long, grinding moment,…” — CLARITY — The ache “all at once
began to drain away” mixes instantaneous onset with gradual process. The contradiction is small, but this
is the peak of the chapter’s central transformation and its tempo should be exact. Fix: Choose either a

[3.037] “On top of the hand itself, his blood magic,…” — POV — The narration steps outside Bal’s

[3.043] “He touched his thumb to each fingertip in turn,…” — CLARITY — “Without ever thinking about

[3.052] “It glowed with a soft inner light, faint but…” — POV — Bal cannot directly know that the sygl

[3.064] “It was very distracting.…” — TELLING — “It was very distracting” labels the effect in generic,

[3.078] “He did know the words.…” — IMAGE — “His heart beat faster” is generic physiological

[3.091] “Said aloud, to a girl at his feet in…” — POV — The sentence explicitly recognizes that the ritual

[3.109] “They sat like that for a few minutes, his…” — CLARITY — “They sat like that” flattens the

[3.116] “"Through the front door, of course," she said, and…” — POV — “Giggled” again casts Amelia in

[3.135] “She said the word lightly, with a sidelong look…” — TELLING — The narration assigns

[3.170] “Through the new thread in his chest he could…” — IMAGE — Something “bright and fizzing”

[3.183] “She stayed facing the road anyway.…” — CLARITY — “Anyway” implies Amelia remains

reason to turn away. The discourse marker creates an opposition the scene has not supplied. Fix: Cut
“anyway” or give her a reason—Bal speaking, the car disappearing—that she overrides.
[3.185] “"My mom died giving birth to me, so they…” — LOGIC — “My mom died giving birth to me, so

feels administratively too neat. Fix: Add the missing operative fact in Bal’s own shorthand—no father
named, no family claimed him, or parish decision.
[3.213] “The schools themselves were on a different plane altogether,…” — CLARITY — A major world

contextually dropped into a walk-and-talk sequence without preparation. Fix: Unpack the plane and

[3.214] “No cars, no phones, no television.…” — REPETITION — “No cars, no phones, no television”

[3.222] “They'd marked out the bases with an old bucket…” — RHYTHM — The softball field is

[3.226] “A small boy with no magic and a very…” — TELLING — The sentence assures the reader that

[3.236] “On the field, the small boy finally caught the…” — RHYTHM — The boy’s hit, takeoff,

Standout sentences
• [3.028] “The end of the stump tingled, then swelled, and something pale…” — The regrowth is

rendered as sequence rather than spectacle: heel, knuckles, fingers. It makes the miracle anatomical and
therefore believable.
• [3.104] “The book had said only that a familiar bond would settle…” — A dry, exact understatement

that measures the distance between textbook language and lived magic; it is also the best summary of

• [3.163] “Hand in hand, they crossed the threshold together…” — Simple syntax, complete symbolic

payoff: the hand that was taken, the hand restored, and the prisoner leaving by the front door.

Editor's chapter notes
Chapter Three has two prose modes. The regrowth and bond sequence is tactile, sequential, and unusually
disciplined: heat moves through the body, the hand assembles in stages, and short fragments mark Bal
testing fingers, light, and pressure. That mode returns in the bond’s pulse and thread. The weaker mode is
explanatory narration stepping ahead of him—most conspicuously the guaranteed power advantage at
3.037 and the school-plane rule at 3.213. Those sentences sound like the author reassuring the reader rather
than Bal experiencing a world whose rules he only partly knows.
Dialogue remains a strength when it is allowed to be plain. Amelia’s “Good as new. Better, in fact” and the
cabbage exchange late in the chapter have more life than the narration’s evaluative labels. But Amelia’s
verbal and physical register is increasingly coy—stretch, caught looking, kneeling, giggling—while Bal’s
body is described through stock beats such as a faster heart at 3.078. The contrast matters: the central
miracle is fresh; the flirtation machinery around it is more conventional.
Sygl — Close Reading · 19

Sentence rhythm opens outward as the chapter proceeds. Inside the temple, short units dominate; outside,
the prose expands into catalogues of courtyard, road, and softball equipment. That expansion suits release
from confinement, but 3.148, 3.222, and 3.236 all rely on long “and” chains. The chapter also repeats
reassurance patterns—Bal does not know what to say, Amelia will explain, the orphanage is not
bad—which are individually natural and cumulatively soften conflict.

Agent's notes
The three-chapter opening now has a complete commercial proposition: Bal receives the hand, the mark,
the goddess-familiar, and admission to the hidden world in one escalating movement. Chapter Three pays
off Chapter Two’s cliffhanger immediately, which is good serial pacing and gives a sample reader a major
transformation before the school plot begins.
It is also the chapter where an acquiring editor would stop and ask the category question in earnest. Bal is
eighteen, but that fact is buried at 3.013; meanwhile Amelia’s erotic teasing, the kneeling bond, and “Time
for that later” push toward adult romantasy while the orphanage, softball game, and school-admission wish
fulfillment pull toward YA. Neither position is untenable, but the manuscript must choose and signal it
cleanly. A second submission concern is consent and bargaining: Bal drinks an ancient goddess’s blood
and accepts a soul bond minutes after learning the basic facts, with Amelia controlling almost all
information. The speed is exciting, but editors will ask whether the novel understands the imbalance or
simply treats it as wish fulfillment.

[4.040] "The inside of Mrs. Brown's office showed little in…" — CLARITY — An office can't "show
personality," and "little in the way of" is padding; the sentence announces a quality the next three
sentences then demonstrate anyway. — Fix: Cut the announcement and open on the filing cabinets.

Sygl — Close Reading · 21

[4.042] "There were two gray filing cabinets against one wall…" — REPETITION — "And that was
nearly all" is the second statement of the room's emptiness in three sentences, before 4.046 makes it a
third. One inventory should carry the point. — Fix: Drop the tail clause; let the list end and speak for itself.
[4.043] "The only thing in the room that belonged to…" — CLARITY — "A picture frame… showing her
husband" momentarily reads as the husband himself being in the room, and the sentence is overloaded —
frame, desk corner, chair orientation, and the reveal all in one breath. — Fix: "a framed photograph of her
husband" and one clause fewer.
[4.046] "Otherwise the office was as spartan and bare as…" — REPETITION — Third pass at the same
observation (4.040, 4.042, now the simile). The waiting-room comparison is the weakest of the three
versions. — Fix: Delete the sentence; 4.047 gets Mrs. Brown on stage sooner.
[4.054] "Everyone's did, and all his life they had always…" — CLARITY — The pronoun "they" refers to
eyes, so "they had always found the same thing there" makes the eyes the finders; "all his life" and
"always" also say the same thing twice. — Fix: "Everyone's did. All his life they had found the same thing
there."
[4.057] "Hoping to forestall a lot of questions he wouldn't…" — TELLING — The narration explains
Bal's motive for summarizing, and then the summary plays out in full dialogue anyway — the explanation
is a stage direction the scene doesn't need. "A lot of questions" is also uncharacteristically vague for this
narrator. — Fix: Cut the preamble, open on his first line.
[4.058] "I saw the roof of the mausoleum flicker the…" — DIALOGUE — The spoken recap that runs
from here to 4.070 is jacket copy, not speech: the entire previous three chapters compressed into tidy,
evenly-weighted clauses by a boy who is supposed to be blurting. It works as exposition and fails as talk.
— Fix: Let him stumble — start with the hand, get interrupted, backfill. Mrs. Brown's reactions are
already written; give her something to react to mid-stream.
[4.072] "Bal couldn't tell what shocked Mrs. Brown more, the…" — CLARITY — "The Vampire
Goddess" appears in the narration as an established title before any character has used it or the text has
defined it; a first-time reader trips on a term the book hasn't issued yet. — Fix: Either seed the epithet in
temple dialogue in Chapter Two or Three, or use plain "goddess" here.
[4.073] "For a good while she kept opening and closing…" — IMAGE — The goldfish comparison is the
reader's first cliché in a chapter otherwise precise about mouths and hands; it also undercuts Mrs. Brown's
dignity a page before the book asks us to take her seriously. — Fix: Describe the motion plainly and let her
laugh at 4.079 do the characterization.
[4.078] "Outside, someone shouted that it was a foul, and…" — TELLING — A shouted dispute is
reported in summary ("several people disagreed") where the chapter's own method — heard sound through
walls and windows — would let one actual shout do the work. — Fix: Give the disagreement one verbatim
cry, as at 4.050.
[4.095] "Once they call roll for any blood sygls, you'll…" — CLARITY — "Call roll for" tangles the
ceremony's mechanics: roll is called by name, not by sygl, and a reader has to rebuild the claiming
procedure from a preposition. — Fix: "When they call for the blood sygls to stand" — or whatever the
actual procedure is, stated in its own terms.
[4.105] "The only reason they haven't outlawed blood sygls from…" — GRAMMAR — You cannot
outlaw people "from attending"; you outlaw attendance. The construction is common in speech but this is
Sygl — Close Reading · 22

Mrs. Brown's most authoritative exposition, and the sentence also states an institutional motive ("want to
be seen as fair") as settled fact. — Fix: "The only reason blood sygls haven't been barred from the schools
is that the schools want to be seen as fair."
[4.146] "Then she turned from the picture back to them…" — REPETITION — The full outfit catalogue
from 4.012 (gray skirt, white blouse, bun) is reissued verbatim to mark Mrs. Brown "becoming herself
again." The transformation is real; the inventory is not needed to prove it — her posture and voice have
already done it. — Fix: One item, not three: the bun, or the skirt smoothed down.
[4.170] "She glanced down at her letter, which in that…" — CLARITY — "Her letter" arrives
unintroduced (she was writing something at 4.047, 120 sentences ago), and the dismissal custom is
explained in the generic second person ("meant you were finished") rather than from Bal's experience of
that office. — Fix: "She glanced down at her writing again — in that office, Bal knew, that meant you
were finished."
[4.183] "As the majority of his belongings were not things…" — RHYTHM — "As the majority of his
belongings were not things of sentimental value" is committee prose in a chapter narrated in plain, warm
declaratives; the Latinate frame ("the majority of") sticks out every time it is read. — Fix: "Most of what
he owned meant nothing to him, so packing didn't take long."
[4.193] "It was a good pocketknife and he liked it…" — TELLING — A verdict with no evidence, in a
packing sequence that elsewhere justifies every kept object (the compass's true needle, the kit's year of
potato-peeling). This one is simply asserted. — Fix: One clause of use or history, or cut the sentence and
let the packing imply it.
[4.205] "Fredrick, the boy who had given them to him…" — CLARITY — "Had been a friend" in the past
perfect quietly implies the friendship is over — dead, moved, estranged — and the text never says which.
A reader will carry an unanswered question the book didn't mean to ask. — Fix: If Fredrick simply left for
another school (confirmed at 6.097), say so here or use the present tense of memory: "Fredrick had given
them to him, and Bal wasn't leaving his books behind."
[4.215] "But she was lying on his bed with her…" — TELLING — The sentence totals up Amelia ("more
like a regular girl than anything out of the stories") immediately before 4.216 makes the same point as a
joke. The summary arrives first and steals the joke's work. — Fix: Cut the verdict; the bed, the
blanket-feet, and the teasing already show it, and 4.216 lands clean.
[4.252] "So Raizen's high priest captured one of mine and…" — CONTINUITY — 4.249 said Raizen "got
one of his followers to kidnap a high priest of mine"; here the kidnapper has become Raizen's high priest
himself. Follower and high priest are different ranks in this world's hierarchy — the book's own plot
machinery (claims, priests, High Priests) depends on the distinction. — Fix: Decide who held the knife and
make both sentences agree; if the escalation is deliberate (a follower then, a priest later), the text must
mark it as two events.
[4.253] "Truthfully, I had other things on my mind at…" — DIALOGUE — A goddess explains that she
was distracted while her high priest was kidnapped and tortured, and the throwaway "Truthfully" plus the
shrug construction make it sound like a missed appointment. If the glibness is meant to chill, it needs one
colder word; as written it reads as the author hurrying past. — Fix: Either let her say it flatly without the
filler adverb, or give Bal one beat of registering what she just admitted.

Sygl — Close Reading · 23

[4.258] "The only reason converting bothers the others is that…" — LOGIC — The sentence claims
converts keeping their old gifts is "the only reason" conversion frightens the originators, but never closes
the loop: a retained gift is an advantage to the convert, not obviously a threat to the gods. The mechanism
(followers defecting with their powers, sygl counts shifting) is one clause away and absent. — Fix: Add
the consequence: "…they keep the gifts — so every convert is a soldier the other side trained."
[4.281] "I doubt it…" — DIALOGUE — Bal's comeback misfires: Amelia says she was locked away
"longer than you'd believe," and "I doubt it" parses as disbelieving her, the opposite of the intended
meaning (that he can believe it). On the page it reads as a small rudeness at the chapter's warmest exit. —
Fix: "Try me" or "I'd believe it" — either keeps the dryness without reversing the sense.

Standout sentences
[4.055] "Today they found a hand." — Five words carrying the whole premise; the paragraph of staring
that precedes it exists to earn this sentence, and it does.
[4.145] "Bal stood there and looked at the man in the frame… and thought that it must have taken her a
great deal longer than a minute to learn to tell it that short." — The chapter's emotional peak, and the
manuscript's best long sentence in this stretch: grief measured by compression. It teaches the reader how to
hear everything Mrs. Brown says afterward.
[4.236] "Bal thought about all the stories told in the dark in this very room, and how little was in any of
them once you took out the screaming." — A whole genre's blood-mage mythology dismissed and
explained in one subordinate clause; funny, and it quietly indicts every campfire tale the reader has ever
enjoyed.

Editor's chapter notes
Rhythm. The chapter's ground rhythm is a competent alternation of medium declaratives with one-line
beats ("She froze." 4.052; "He was dead." 4.044; "It was blood." 4.134) — a reliable engine, and the
one-line beats are well spaced until the office scene, where they cluster (4.098–4.101, 4.104) and the
staccato starts to feel like a mannerism rather than an emphasis. The long sentences are the chapter's glory
and its risk in the same places: 4.145 earns every clause; 4.043 and 4.215 spend the same syntax on
inventory and summary.
Repeated constructions. Three tics to watch. (1) The certified verdict: the narrator repeatedly announces a
quality and then demonstrates it anyway — the bare office three times (4.040, 4.042, 4.046), Amelia's
regular-girl status twice in two sentences (4.215–4.216). (2) Generic second person in narration — "as you
walked on them" (4.003), "you got a severe punishment" (4.014), "meant you were finished" (4.170) — a
voice the POV chapters elsewhere avoid, and it always appears when the text is summarizing custom
rather than showing scene. (3) Pen-tapping and mouth-thinness as Mrs. Brown's only two thinking gestures
(4.091, 4.098, 4.106) — fine twice, a tell by the third.
Dialogue. Bal's voice is the most consistent thing in the chapter — dry, literal, kind ("why get upset when
you were?", 4.016) — and Amelia's teasing register is already distinct from her formal one. The weak
stretch is functional dialogue: Mrs. Brown's admissions exposition (4.095–4.105) is a rulebook wearing a
cardigan, and Bal's opening summary (4.058–4.070) is the book's whole premise recited as small talk. Both
scenes have a human being across the desk who could interrupt, misunderstand, or react mid-stream; the
chapter uses that resource only after the information is delivered (the laugh at 4.079).

Sygl — Close Reading · 24

Telling vs. showing. The chapter knows how to show — the fist opening finger by finger under Amelia's
hand (4.150–4.161), the rug restored to its floorboards (4.176–4.178), the husband's story told in under a
minute (4.131–4.144). Its telling problem is not ignorance but impatience: verdicts are issued before or
after scenes that were about to prove them, as if the draft didn't trust itself. A revision pass that simply
deleted flagged verdict-sentences — 4.040, 4.046, 4.179-type summaries, 4.215 — would lose almost no
information and gain measurable pace.
Filter words. Light and mostly honest: "Bal guessed" he could not tell (4.072), "he figured" — the filters
here mark real uncertainty. The exception is 4.057's motive-announcing participle ("Hoping to
forestall…"), which filters the whole summary scene through intention before it happens.

Agent's notes
Chapter Four is the manuscript's character chapter, and viewed commercially it does essential work: it
proves the book can generate feeling without the temple machinery — a widowed schoolmistress's
backstory (4.131–4.145) is the first scene an editor will dog-ear, because it converts the world's prejudice
from set-dressing into a body count. Mrs. Brown is also the book's first credible adult, which matters for
the YA-crossover shelf this is aimed at: the adults here have histories, not just functions.
The flags an acquiring editor would raise on this stretch are about control, not talent. First, the
recapitulation problem: Bal's summary to Mrs. Brown (4.058–4.070) tells an acquiring editor the author is
anxious the reader won't follow — in a fourth chapter, that anxiety reads as inexperience, and the scene's
own comedy (her laugh, the goldfish beat) proves the material can carry itself. Second, the 4.249/4.252
rank contradiction: in a book whose entire power system is denominated in priests and High Priests, an
editor will treat a rank slip as a systems error, not a typo, and it will cost trust disproportionate to its size.
Third, the enrollment mechanics arrive entirely as dialogue exposition; an editor will ask for one
procedural fact to be dramatized (a letter written on-page, a seal, a refusal) so the school's gatekeeping
feels like an institution rather than an explanation.
Against that: the chapter's sellable asset is restraint. The husband's story, the fist under the hand, the rug
left behind — this is a writer who can do quiet. Quiet is what's scarce in this category, and it's why this
chapter, flags and all, raises the manuscript's value rather than merely advancing its plot.

[5.102] "Verin hummed for a while and then stopped…" — RHYTHM — A pure time-passing sentence:

[5.104] "Going to this school had been all Bal had…" — TELLING — "All Bal had ever dreamed about"

arriving here as flat narration, it inflates rather than deepens. — Fix: One concrete childhood instance (a
name day, a watched departure) outsells the superlative.
[5.108] "They had never been allowed into any of the magykal…" — TELLING — A paragraph's worth of

[5.130] "Once you're officially claimed, your sygl will appear on…" — DIALOGUE — "So don't worry

[5.157] "I haven't had human blood in a very long…" — GRAMMAR — "I need it to be at my full

[5.158] "I'm worried I won't be able to stop the others…" — CLARITY — The trailing "without it" lands
grammatically on "the others" — as though the other originators might try something without blood —

[5.172] "He hugged her back anyway, a beat late, and…" — RHYTHM — Five actions chained with "and"

[5.176] "It was information Bal would never have gotten anywhere…" — TELLING — The narration

letting us overhear one precious sentence of it. — Fix: Quote one line of her account verbatim; the chapter
can spare the space.
[5.182] "Watching Amelia now, he thought this was what he…" — TELLING — "What he wanted in a

[5.191] "He ladled himself a bowl, sat down in the one…" — TELLING — Verin "began to explain what

[5.209] "They know that once you leave school, a lot…" — DIALOGUE — Institutional sociology in the

Standout sentences
[5.044] "In eighteen years it had not occurred to him that Mrs. Brown had a first name." — An entire
childhood of institutional life measured in one oversight; funny and quietly devastating at once.
[5.262] "He didn't wipe his thumb on anything." — After wiping Amelia's blood tear, Bal keeps the smear
on his own skin. Devotion rendered as a declined action; the best sentence in the chapter.
[5.305] "Her fingers were warmer than they had been in the buggy, almost as warm as his own." — The
feeding's whole meaning paid off as a temperature: his blood, her warmth. No commentary could do what
this measurement does.

Editor's chapter notes
Rhythm and shape. This is the manuscript's most confident chapter so far at the sentence level: a
deliberate alternation of short declarative beats (5.017–5.019 "It was still there. He got up.") against long,
loaded descriptive sentences (5.079, 5.120, 5.226, 5.294). The long sentences earn their length because
they carry inventory or sensation rather than qualification. Where the rhythm sags is in the chapter's
middle exposition: Verin's briefing (5.191–5.212) flattens into uniform medium-length procedural
sentences, and the prose's pulse measurably drops until Amelia's shaking shoulders (5.203) — the one
human irregularity — arrives.
Repeated constructions. The chapter leans on the "X, and [contrast]" hinge — "He was another of the
orphans, and…", "The muffin was a little stale and he ate all of it anyway" — usually to good, dry effect.
A more noticeable tic is the certified-summary sentence that follows a shown beat: "It was information Bal
would never have gotten anywhere else" (5.176), "Going to this school had been all Bal had ever dreamed
about" (5.104), "this was what he wanted in a girl" (5.182). Three times in one chapter the narrator steps in
to grade a feeling the scene already scored. It is the chapter's single recurring habit worth breaking.
Dialogue. Verin is the find: a voice built from courtesies and deflections ("Please don't take too long
saying your goodbyes"), and his "Sarah" (5.041) does more world-building than the portal speech. The
dialogue flags cluster where characters recite systems — Amelia's brand statute (5.061), the
claiming/uniform rules (5.130), Verin's curriculum sociology (5.209). The pattern is consistent: whenever
a character explains how the world works, the sentence count of hedges and clauses doubles. Bal and
Amelia's exchange around the feeding request is the counterproof — short lines, everything important in
the pauses (5.283–5.285), and the consent beat at 5.284 ("Are you sure?" asked after the wrist is raised) is
Sygl — Close Reading · 28

the best-written moment in the chapter.
Filter words and telling. "Bal guessed" (5.141), "if Bal had to guess" (5.075), "He figured" (5.114) — the
filters are present but honest, marking inference as inference, which keeps the POV clean. The telling
problem is not filters; it's the narrator's habit of sealing scenes with verdicts, flagged at 5.104, 5.176, and
5.182 above.
The feeding scene itself. Sentences 5.286–5.298 are the tightest prose in the chapter: sensation rationed
(pressure, cool hands, hair, sound), Bal's talking used as a pressure valve, and the closure of the wounds
(5.297–5.298) described in plain sequence. The two grammar flags at 5.157–5.158 matter precisely
because they occur in the request, where the chapter can least afford a stumble — a reader's attention is at
its peak and the pronoun error is at the emotional center.

Agent's notes
This chapter is the one an acquiring editor quotes back to the author. The portal arrival, the cottage, the
bench under the stars, and a feeding scene that is intimate rather than titillating — it demonstrates the book
can do tenderness at the same standard as its premise. If the manuscript were being positioned, Chapter
Five is the "read one more" chapter that converts a partial request into a full.
Commercially, the stretch confirms category: this is YA-crossover fantasy romance in structure — an
eighteen-year-old lead, a powerful not-quite-human love interest, an academy ahead — and Chapter Five
is where the romance engine visibly turns over (5.182, softened or not; 5.289; 5.305). An acquiring editor
will note the book knows what it is.
Submission flags for this stretch are modest and all line-level: (1) the 5.157 pronoun error sits in the book's
most important early scene and must not survive another draft — errors in peak scenes are what editors
remember; (2) the cover-story logic at 5.062 is asserted rather than built, and editors read for exactly that
kind of hand-wave because it predicts later ones; (3) the exposition-by-lecture pattern (5.108, 5.191) is
tolerable in a fifth chapter and would not be in a fifteenth — worth the author knowing now. None of it
touches the chapter's marketability. This is the strongest of the three chapters in this assignment, and it isn't
close.
